  - **Refusals, in order:** `unauthorized` (no session), then the account's standing, asked **before** the session is looked at — `not_platform_account` (no active staff row — a company user, or a disabled one), `account_blocked`, `no_account` — then `reauth_required` (no fresh password proof), then the request itself: `rate_limited` / `same_email` / `email_taken`. `email_taken` keeps the shape the code path already answers, so the browser cannot tell them apart.
  - **Not verified here:** the RLS kit (no Docker on the dev machine) and staging. Run `npm run test:rls` before merging; the new file is `tests/rls/platform-self-email-change.sql`.
  - **On the merged tree (#65 and #56 are both in), it redefines nothing either of them redefines**, and both new functions ask `app_private.platform_staff_role_of_account(user_id)` — the **account fact** of `20260919130000`, not the session-gated `platform_staff_role_of`. That is the right one twice over: the question is whether the row exists, and the callers reach it only through the service role, whose key carries a role and no `sub`, so `jwt_has_subject()` is false and the gate would not fire either way (`tests/rls/platform-amr.sql`: "the service role still reads the staff role"). Self-check 4c fails a release that swaps them back — a silent no-op today, and a silent NULL the first time the caller holds a subject. `audit_write`'s signature is unchanged by #65 (the same eleven parameters; only the `actor_role` lookup moved to the same account helper), and the call passes ten positionally, leaving `p_occurred_at` to default as it must for a non-`legacy` category.
- **What comes next in phase 3, and why 3.4.** The owner approved **slice 3.4 (support sessions)** as the next one, ahead of 3.6a, 3.12, 3.7, 3.8 and 3.10–3.13. The reason belongs in the record: on the same day he approved 3.3's temporary concession, under which any staff role holding `business.read` reads every company's Fahrtenbuch, planning, historical and ledger data directly, **with no audit row and no session**. 3.4 is what closes it, so the longer it waits the longer that concession stands. **3.6a has since merged as PR #57**, so what remains after 3.4 is 3.12, 3.7a/b, 3.8 U1–U3, 3.10, 3.11 and 3.13. Phase 3 still counts 6 of 13: 3.6a, like 3.6c before it, lands inside the 3.6 slice already counted at PR #36, and inflating the tally for a slice that was counted once would be the wrong kind of progress. **In flight right now:** PR #56 (3.4a, the support-session database and functions) is **open and in rework, not merged**; PR #58 (3.10, first half — the seller, the trial length and the invoice defaults), PR #59 (the forgot-password spec's timeout budget) and PR #69 (4.3d, a cash salary payment becomes a Gehaltsquittung) are open.
- **The four panel pages (PR #50, plan slice 3.6c), and the Konto link (PR #48), what to know:**
  - Verzeichnisse (vehicles, drivers and users across all companies), Protokoll (the audit log), Einstellungen (the price book and the plan feature map) and Team. They read the read models and write RPCs phase 3 already serves; no new SQL.
  - Four major findings from the review, all fixed before merge — including that following the Konto link dropped a platform account into the *tenant* shell, and that the `role_requires_3_3` wording had become false now that 3.3 has shipped.
  - ~~**Recorded honestly: the Team roster has no read model.** It is reconstructed from the audit log, so it identifies members by user id rather than address and cannot show who granted a membership.~~ — closed by PR #87 (slice 3.12, `platform_staff_list`), above.
  - ~~**Therefore audit retention (decision D4) must not be implemented before 3.12 ships that read model.**~~ — the fold is gone with PR #87; D4 still must not ship before that PR is **live on production**. It is, since 21.09.2026, and D4 is built — see its entry below.
  - 3.6c is done apart from the support-session workspace, which belongs to slice 3.4 and does not exist yet.
- **The Google registration gate (PR #51), what to know:**
  - Google sign-in was switched on for staging and the owner hit a live bug within minutes: he signed up with Google and landed inside the tenant shell at `/overview` with a company-less account and `invalid input syntax for type uuid: "null"` errors, instead of the "complete your company registration" screen. `needsCompanyRegistration` read a missing profile as "nothing to do".
  - **The finding worth carrying forward: there is no trigger that creates `user_profiles` rows.** `auth.users` carries only `mark_pending_oauth_signup` and `sync_user_profile_email`, and `public.handle_new_user` exists with nothing invoking it. The profile is the signup RPCs' own work. Two places in the repository assert otherwise, and one of them is `tests/rls/oauth-signup.sql`, whose prelude **creates the trigger itself** — so the RLS kit manufactured the premise it relied on and could never have caught its absence.
  - The owner's stuck test account was discarded cleanly afterwards, and he can retry.
- **One sign-in screen (the owner's decision of 17.09.2026) — this reverses half of PR #57.**
  - **`/login` is the only place anyone signs in**, platform accounts and company users alike, and the account decides where the session goes afterwards: `/admin` for a platform account, `/overview` or the panel page it was heading for otherwise. `/admin/login` is **not deleted** — it redirects onto `/login`, carrying the remembered return path — and `PlatformLoginPage.tsx` is gone with the nine `admin.login.*` strings it was the only reader of. Routing after sign-in is unchanged; only the door is.
  - **`SignInRoute` is registered in both routers** (`src/App.tsx`), for the same reason `AdminLoginRoute` was: signing in swaps the public router for the authenticated one, which starts at whatever the address bar still says, so the redirect has to be a route and not a `navigate()` inside the form.
  - **The Google button shows for everyone**, because nothing knows who a visitor is before they sign in. A platform account that presses it is refused, its session ended, and the screen says why — `Verwaltungskonten melden sich nur mit E-Mail und Passwort an.` / `Administration accounts sign in with email and password only.` The decision is one pure function, `googleSignInRefusal()` in `src/lib/adminGate.ts`, and it asks **`sessionCameFromGoogle`** — "this session was minted by Google" — never "this account has a Google identity", or a platform account whose address also exists at Google would be signed out of a correct password sign-in.
  - **A platform account sent back by `SetPasswordPage` now arrives with a sentence.** Its session came from a one-time code, so it holds no capability and has to be replaced by a password sign-in — and the one sign-in screen is the company card, which says nothing about the platform. Without a notice the account landed on a generic form with no idea why. It sets `auth.notice.platformPasswordSignIn` through the new `setAuthNotice` on AuthContext; the notice lives above `App` and outside `clearSessionData`, so it survives the sign-out and the router swap, and `stayPut` (the sign-out-failed path) takes it back so a still-signed-in session never leaves a message waiting for the next visitor.
  - **The browser's refusal is the message, not the wall.** Since `20260919130000` the database answers NULL to every platform capability asked from a session GoTrue did not mint with a password, so a Google session gets nothing either way. Do not "simplify" the browser check away on the grounds that the server covers it: it does, and the account then sees an empty panel instead of a sentence.
  - **What this widens, in three measured facts rather than in reassurance.** The first version of this entry was wrong in both directions; this is the corrected one.
    1. **The network surface is unchanged.** `PlatformLoginPage` posted to the same `/auth/v1/token?grant_type=password` through the same `AuthContext.signIn`, so the endpoint a platform password is guessed against was never path-gated. What widens is **UI discoverability** and **credential stuffing by default** — a bot that posts to the one form it can see now reaches platform accounts too.
    2. **The distinct refusal messages do not reveal whether an address exists.** #57's uniform-refusal property is **gone** — `/login` branches into a block reason, `emailNotConfirmed` (with its "E-Mail bestätigen" CTA), `tooManyAttempts`, `network` and `generic` — but it was not protecting anything obtainable: measured on staging 17.09.2026 against a throwaway unconfirmed `@example.com` account, GoTrue v2.170.0 verifies the password **first** (wrong password → `400 invalid_credentials`; correct password → `400 email_not_confirmed`; unknown address → `400 invalid_credentials`). The block-reason branch needs the credentials too — `AuthContext.signIn` returns early on GoTrue's error and only then asks `checkProfileAccess`. **Do not restore the uniform message believing it closes an oracle.**
    3. **What remains is the rate limit's shape, and it is narrow.** `GOTRUE_RATE_LIMIT_HEADER: X-Client-IP`, with Caddy overwriting that header from the connecting address, so the budget is **per source IP**: `RATE_LIMIT_TOKEN` (60) requests per 5 minutes on `/token`, **shared between password sign-ins and refresh-token calls**, with **no per-account counter and no lockout anywhere**. It bounds a single-host brute force and scales away with rotating IPs, and because the budget is shared, a guesser behind the same NAT as real users can break *their* token refresh. The "52 wrong passwords, the 53rd 429" measurement is one IP against one budget; it does not measure per-account protection, because there is none. **If per-account protection is wanted, it is new work and nothing in this PR provides it.**
  - **The e2e suite still has no spec that signs in as a platform account**, because staging has no platform test account: making one goes through `/admin/team` → invite an `@example.com` address → read the code in Mailpit → `/einladung`, and `setup-staging.sh` does none of it. **Expect the code-minted session to hold no capability and be signed out to `/login`** — that is `20260919130000` working, not a bug. The three new specs in `e2e/tests/sign-in.spec.ts` therefore check the routing that needs no session, and read the remembered path out of `window.history.state.usr` — the only way to observe it, since it never touches the address bar.
  - **Two things the adversarial review of #74 corrected, worth keeping:**
    - **A sign-out that fails as a *value* used to leave the session on screen.** `supabase.auth.signOut()` POSTs `/logout` and returns early with `{ error }` for any failure that is not 404/401/403 or a missing session — **before** `_removeSession()` — so a retryable fetch failure left both the React state and the stored session in place, and `AuthContext.signOut` only read the thrown case (read from the installed source, `@supabase/auth-js` 2.98.0, `GoTrueClient._signOut`). On the refusal path that meant a platform account keeping `user`, the authenticated router staying mounted, the address saying `/login`, and no sentence anywhere — the exact empty panel the refusal exists to prevent. Both shapes are now handled, and the stored session is removed here rather than left to a call that cannot do it offline: `scope: 'local'` is **not** a network-free path (`GoTrueAdminApi.signOut` makes the same request whatever the scope), so the key is removed directly and is named once, as `AUTH_STORAGE_KEY` in `src/lib/supabase.ts`. **Untested:** there is no DOM test environment for `AuthContext` (vitest runs in node), so this is source-read and reasoned, not covered.
    - **`SIGN_IN_PATH` is for the address rewrites, and `CompleteRegistrationPage` was the one that had been missed.** The remaining `<Link to="/login">` in LandingPage, SignupPage, VerifyEmailPage, ForgotPasswordPage and AuthCallbackPage are deliberately left as literals: the constant exists because every place that rewrites the address *around a router swap* must agree, and a `<Link>` is an ordinary navigation. Converting them is a cosmetic sweep across files other PRs are touching.
- **The `/admin` front door (PR #57, plan slice 3.6a) — read this before starting 3.13.**
  - **The panel's URL changed.** It is registered **once**, under `/admin`. Its own sign-in at `/admin/login` lasted one day and is now a redirect onto `/login` (see the entry above). `/super-admin/*` keeps working as a rewrite onto `/admin/*`, subpath, query and hash preserved, so there is one panel and not two copies of the route table that could drift.
  - **Where the rewrite actually lives**, because the name is easy to get wrong: it is `legacyAdminTarget()` in `src/lib/adminGate.ts`, used by the `LegacyAdminRedirect` and `PlatformSignInRedirect` components in `src/App.tsx`. It is **not** `PLATFORM_ACCOUNT_REDIRECTS`, which is a different, one-entry map (`src/App.tsx:182`, `{'/mein-konto': '/admin/konto'}`) that `PlatformAccountGuard` uses to keep a platform account out of the tenant shell. The two are unrelated; slice 3.13 removes the first.
  - The in-app links that still say `/super-admin` (`OverviewPage.tsx:491`, `Sidebar.tsx:146`, `UserMenu.tsx:19`, and `SuperAdminDashboard.tsx`'s own links) are deliberately left alone and work through the rewrite. **3.13 deletes the rewrite together with those links** — doing one without the other breaks the panel.
  - **The remembered return path is validated, and that is not a formality:** a value that decides where a browser goes after signing in is an open redirect the moment it is taken on trust. `platformReturnPath()` (`src/lib/adminGate.ts`) keeps only a path inside the panel — an absolute URL, a protocol-relative `//host`, a backslash the browser may read as a separator and any `..` segment are all dropped, and either sign-in path is dropped too, because each would hand the path straight back. The path travels in the router's own location state, never the query string, so it stays out of the address bar. **Since the unification it changes hands twice** — `PlatformSignInRedirect` or `/admin/login` remembers it, `SignInRoute` follows it — and `rememberedPanelPath()` re-validates at **each** step: nothing about a value checked once makes it safe the second time it is used.
  - None of it is a security boundary. `platform_can()` and RLS check every platform action again in the database, and `user_profiles.is_super_admin` is read here for routing only — it has granted nothing since slice 3.1.
- **The recurring `forgot-password` e2e failure has two causes, and only one of them is fixed.**
  - **Fixed (PR #55): the “cold-boot” 502 — and the name is wrong.** `POST /functions/v1/otp-request` answers **502 in about 130 ms**, but measurement later showed the cause is **the gateway dialling a stale container address after a release**, not a cold worker, and idleness does not trigger it. The warm-up added here on the cold-worker belief therefore proves less than it claims. Kept as written below for the history, with this correction at the head of it. Originally attributed to a cold edge worker, and `src/lib/otpClient.ts` maps a 5xx with no `ok` field to `internal`, so the screen showed its error box 0.45 s after the click while the assertion waited out its full timeout. No timeout can wait out an answer that already came, which is why PR #33 (20 s on that assertion) and PR #40 (15 s on every expect) both failed to help. A warm-up in global setup (`e2e/lib/warmup.ts`) now boots the stack before the first assertion.
  - **Open, and a test-design defect rather than a product one: the product's own limit of five codes per address per hour.** `c_email_hourly CONSTANT integer := 5` (`supabase/migrations/20260915100100_otp_rpcs.sql:57`) refuses the sixth request for an address within the hour, and `otp-request` answers the public purposes with **202 either way, by design** — so the suite is told the code was sent, and then waits for mail that was never sent. **Evidence, read-only on staging:** `app_private.otp_send_events` holds three `email_hourly` refusals for `e2e-admin@example.com` on 16.09.2026, at 16:22:46, 16:47:47 and 16:57:46 UTC, each with exactly five `issued` rows in the hour before it; 17 `issued` and 3 `email_hourly` for that address over six hours.
  - **The fix must not weaken the limit** — it is a real anti-abuse control on a public endpoint. It belongs in the suite: a fresh address per run, or a wait, or reading the refusal rather than the 202. PR #59 is open on this spec but addresses the *timeout budget*, not this; it does note in passing that a run inside the cap is indistinguishable from a broken mailer.
- **Still open, and a product defect rather than a test one: the cold-boot 502 itself.**
  - A release is not what makes the worker cold, only what guarantees it: the same 502 was reproduced after **ten quiet minutes with no deploy involved**. So anyone whose "Passwort vergessen" is the first traffic in a while meets it too, and sees "Ein unerwarteter Fehler ist aufgetreten" for a request that was never made.
  - **Production does not have this today only because it has no `otp-request`.** Its deployed bundle predates the pipeline — `/srv/PRO/releases/current/supabase/functions` holds `company-signup`, `extract-receipt`, `generate-activation-code`, `main`, `manage-users`, `update-income-weeks` and `validate-discount-code`, and none of `otp-request`, `otp-verify`, `oauth-account` or `platform-staff`. **The first production release brings the function and this behaviour together**, so weigh it before pressing 0.6.
- **The Gehaltsquittung preview on the salary payment row (PR #49), what to know:**
  - The row already showed a lock badge and a link that navigated away to Dokumente. It now previews the stored receipt in place, with a PDF download.
  - Its review found and fixed a defect where opening a second receipt painted the previous one's document for a frame.
- **Changed on the hosts on 16.09.2026, all with the owner's approval:**
  - **Production GoTrue signup was open and is now closed.** It reported `disable_signup:false` with `mailer_autoconfirm:true`, and production has no Kong `auth-v1-closed` block because it has never run a pipeline release — re-probed on 16.09.2026, a bodyless POST to `/auth/v1/signup`, `/otp` and `/magiclink` answers **400** on production, meaning it reaches GoTrue, against **403** on staging, where Kong terminates it. (An earlier note here said 405; that was a differently shaped probe.) It now reports `disable_signup:true`, verified end to end: a wrong-password login still returns `invalid_credentials`, and a signup attempt returns `422 signup_disabled`. **Not verified by exploiting it — no account was created on production.** The trap for anyone repeating this: production's compose reads the key as `DISABLE_SIGNUP`, not `GOTRUE_DISABLE_SIGNUP`.
  - **Google sign-in is on for staging**, verified: `/auth/v1/settings` reports `google: true`, and Kong still answers 403 on `/auth/v1/signup`, `/otp` and `/magiclink`. **Production stays off, and must, until the server-side `amr` rule exists** — the refusal of a platform-staff Google sign-in is enforced in the browser only.
  - **`OTP_PEPPER` was written for production**: 48 characters, different from staging's, file mode 600. It was the last technical blocker on step 0.6. Nothing was restarted, since the containers read it at deploy time. Re-checked on the host 16.09.2026 — `/srv/PRO/deploy/.env` is mode 600, the value is 48 characters, staging's is 64, and the two differ (compared by hash on the host; neither value was printed, and neither should be).
  - **The staging platform owner is now `super@professor.com`.** The previous `admin@yamobility.de` was **disabled, not deleted**: a hard delete fails with `23503` on `activation_codes_created_by_fkey` — that account created real activation codes — and forcing it would destroy real history. Confirmed read-only on staging: `app_private.platform_staff` holds exactly two rows, `super@professor.com` as an active `owner` and `admin@yamobility.de` as an `owner` with `disabled_at = 2026-09-16 12:52:28 UTC`. **Staging only; production was not touched.**
- **3.1 platform authority (PR #24), what to know:**
  - Until 3.3 rewrites the older policies, `is_user_super_admin()` (any active staff row) gives every staff role owner-level writes. Grant no non-owner role on production before 3.3, and 3.5 must refuse creating one until then.
  - The production backfill makes every flagged profile an owner and prints how many of them still have a company. Staging had one owner and none with a company.
  - The platform_login eligibility inside `otp_issue` now reads the staff row. The Phase 1 platform_login objects stay unused, since 3.2 was cancelled.
  - Follow-up for 3.5: `platform_audit_record` lets any active staff role write unlimited rows attributed to itself; add a rate limit or restrict the outcome.
  - D2, D3 and D5 keep their conservative defaults. Rerun a single spec with `npx playwright test forgot-password` (a name filter; a Windows path argument finds no tests).
  - The laptop has 8 GB RAM. Agents run every `npm ci` and build through `buildlock.sh` in the session scratchpad (two machine-wide slots) and wrap RLS kit runs on the VPS in `flock /root/.el-professor-rls-kit.lock`.
- **Second security finding, 15.09.2026.** `create_user_profile_manual` was executable by anon.
  - **Why the guard failed:** it read `IF NOT is_user_super_admin()`, and the helper returned NULL for a caller without a profile. On top of that, `enforce_user_profile_privilege_guard` returns NEW when `auth.uid()` is NULL.
  - **No role changed:** the upsert still failed with 23502, because `user_profiles.email` is NOT NULL and the function never supplies it.
  - **Closed by hand, with the owner's approval:** EXECUTE revoked on production and staging (anon gets 401).
  - **Made permanent (PR #26, `20260915130000`):** it also makes `is_user_super_admin()` answer false, never NULL.
  - **Follow-ups from its scan:**
    - anon still holds EXECUTE on `reassign_fahrtenbuch_trip`, `delete_activation_code`, `hide_activation_code`, `revoke_activation_code` and `update_company_subscription_manual`. Their guards are NULL-safe; PR #28 (`20260915140000`) revoked anon and PUBLIC on all five.
    - `create_user_profile_manual` can never complete, and nothing calls `list_users_for_admin` or `create_user_account`. Drop them with a `SCHEMA_EPOCH` bump.
    - `preview_activation_code` compares its binding with a NULL-blind `<>` (3.5).
    - `enforce_user_profile_privilege_guard` passes any caller whose `auth.uid()` is NULL (3.1).
- **Function ownership, found in the PR #25 review.**
  - On the real databases, older SECURITY DEFINER functions such as `redeem_activation_code` are owned by `postgres`, which is not a superuser there.
  - `CREATE OR REPLACE` keeps that owner. A redefined function that calls an `app_private` helper then fails with "permission denied", while the RLS kit passes, because its fixture has no owners.
  - Add `ALTER FUNCTION … OWNER TO CURRENT_USER` and an owner self-check (see `phase3/platform-authority`, migration `20260916110010`).
  - **PR #62 rebuilt the kit's simulation of that owner.** `tests/fixtures/owner-simulation.sql` stays opt-in (`RLS_BEFORE` + `RLS_BEFORE_SQL`) and hands the five planning RPCs to `kit_weak_owner`, a non-superuser mirroring the real `postgres` attribute for attribute — `BYPASSRLS`, `INHERIT`, member of `pg_read_all_data` and the three API roles, and weak only in EXECUTE. It now **asserts** its own weakness instead of printing it: EXECUTE on 0 of the `app_private` functions and USAGE held, or it raises. A migration granting EXECUTE on that schema's functions to a role it inherits would otherwise empty the simulation in silence.
  - **What it does not reach, measured on 16.09.2026 rather than argued.** `20260919120000`'s grant loop (`:746-773`) and its self-check 6 (`:981-989`) are **not** exercised: delete that whole grant block and a kit run at that position still passes (exit 0). None of the three callers of `app_private.signup_trial_days()` can be handed over, because `prosrc` carries the DECLARE section where `:612` puts the call, so a weak owner trips the identity rule in `tests/rls/platform-authority.sql:330-339` — measured, exit 1, `FAIL: every SECURITY DEFINER function that references app_private belongs to its owner or a superuser`. Dropping `app_private.signup_insert_company` from the fixture was required for a green run, not a matter of taste; staging owns it with `supabase_admin`, not `postgres`. `20260917130000` self-check 8 still cannot fail either, for any owner that is an `authenticated` member — which every real one is.
  - **The contradiction underneath is open, and it belongs to the migration rather than to the test.** `20260919120000:119-128` deliberately supports a foreign owner by granting it USAGE and EXECUTE by name — a **privilege** rule — where `platform-authority.sql:330-339`, `20260916110010:1022-1034` and `20260916130000:1489-1500` assert an **identity** rule. The two migration checks sort before it and never see the new body, so only the test catches it. They collide the day anyone hands that function to `postgres`. Converting the test would also leave it disagreeing with two migrations already applied under schema epoch 2, so it was left alone and written down.
- **Subscription conflict report (PR #21), counts on staging:**
  - 9 companies say `active` with no expiry, 8 of them suspended.
  - 2 still say `active` after their expiry.
  - 1 paying company is locked because its status is still `trial`.
  - 11 have suspension flags that disagree.
  - 1 subscription row is invoiced at a price that differs from the price book.
  - 2 open invoices are past due while access is active.
  - 1 old activation code has no recorded period.
  - ~~U1 needs an owner rule (D8 to D10) for each class.~~ **Answered on 17.09.2026 — the six rules are in the next entry.**
- **One subscription model (plan 3.F step U1), what to know.** The owner answered every open rule on 17.09.2026, and they are written here because the next person will look for them here rather than in a migration header:
  1. **A company that is `active` with no expiry stays open.** It is carried across as *open-ended* coverage and gets no expiry. **Operational note, not code: before anyone lifts a suspension on such a company, a human sets an expiry by hand.** Otherwise reactivating it grants open-ended access again, which is exactly what rule 1 says it already had.
  2. **A company `active` past its expiry is written `expired`.** A stored-word correction only; both gates already refused it.
  3. **One suspension state: `companies.status = 'suspended'`.** `approval_status` and `subscription_status` no longer carry the value, and their CHECK constraints refuse it. Two live holes close with it: `platform_company_lifecycle('reactivate')` resets only `status`, so a company suspended through one of the other two columns stayed locked with nothing on screen explaining why; and a company suspended only through `subscription_status` could be un-suspended by its own administrator redeeming any valid activation code (`20260916130020:430` set it to `'active'` unconditionally).
  4. **A subscription row keeps the price it was assigned.** Money in the new structures is integer cents.
  5. **Invoices do not affect access in U1.** Paying extends nothing, being overdue locks nothing; `subscription_coverage` holds no `invoice` row at all. That is U2's, and U2 needs a grace-days number the owner has not given.
  6. **An old activation code with no recorded period gets no reconstructed period.** Nothing is guessed from `used_at`.
  - **What ships:** `public.subscription_coverage` (the periods a company is paid or granted, with their source) and `app_private.recompute_company_subscription`, the only writer of `companies.subscription_status` and `.subscription_expires_at`. It derives `.plan` as well, but `platform_company_set_plan` writes that column *and* the coverage rows together — a company with no coverage has no period for a tier to be derived from, and the screen must still be able to set one. Three writers now record a period and let the recompute write those three columns back: `redeem_activation_code`, `update_company_subscription_manual` and — for the tier only — `platform_company_set_plan`. `platform_company_set_approval('suspend')` writes `companies.status` instead of `approval_status`.
  - **This is not a server-only slice, and an earlier draft of this entry said it was.** Moving suspension onto one column only works if that is the column the client reads, and it was not: `AuthContext.checkProfileAccess()` reads `companies.status` but runs only on `signIn`, while `loadUserProfile` — the path every reload and token refresh takes — selected `approval_status` and `subscription_status` and **not** `status`. Left alone, a suspended company would have walked through App.tsx's gate 5 into `/overview` and been told by `LockedFeaturePage` that its *plan* was too low, and `CompanySuspendedPage` would have become dead code. `AuthContext` now carries `companyStatus`, read in the same two queries, and the gate asks it.
  - **Four behaviour changes, and nothing else moves for a customer:**
    1. A company whose suspension had been carried by `subscription_status` now sees **`CompanySuspendedPage`** instead of `/abo-aktivieren`. Both block; the new one names the real reason.
    2. **Redeeming a code on an open-ended company no longer caps it at `now() + 1 month`.** Before, a company with no expiry lost its open-ended access the moment it redeemed anything. Rule 1 says it stays open, and `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` pins it.
    3. **`update_company_subscription_manual` stores the derived word, not the picked one** — `expired` with a future end is stored `inactive`, `inactive` with a past end is stored `expired`, and `suspended` becomes whichever of those two the dates make it. **None of the four grants**, so access is unchanged for every pick, but `ManualSubscriptionModal` re-opens on the stored word. The trail row in `subscription_admin_actions` records the stored word too, and the platform audit row keeps `requested_status` beside it. **A picked `suspended` does not suspend the company** and never writes `companies.status`: that modal is the Abo-Status, suspending is `companies.lifecycle`'s act (owners-only under D2) while this RPC asks `billing.manage`, and an earlier draft that wrote the status handed billing an owner's act, one-way, while storing `active` for a company it had just blocked.
    4. **Reactivating from the Freigaben table returns a company to `active`, not to `trial`.** `platform_company_lifecycle('reactivate')` writes `status = 'active'` unconditionally and always has, for CompaniesPage's lifecycle menu; what is new is that suspending from Freigaben now writes `companies.status`, so that path reaches it too. A company suspended during a running trial comes back `active` and stops getting the trial's premium although `trial_end` is ahead. The server admits both words, so access is unchanged. **Not fixed here because restoring the prior status needs the prior status, and neither suspending path records it** — only the audit row's `before.status` has it. Whether to build that is a decision for the measurement below.
  - **What a platform administrator will notice:** a company suspended from the Freigaben table on `SuperAdminDashboard` now shows a red **Gesperrt** badge there and offers **Reaktivieren**. That table has no lifecycle column — it renders one badge from `approval_status` — so it had to learn to read `companies.status`, or it would have shown a suspended company as green *Genehmigt*, kept offering *Sperren*, and lost the way back (its reactivate button was keyed on an `approval_status` value that no longer occurs).
  - **Measured read-only on production (live, `600b77b`, 19 companies, 17.09.2026):**
    - `subscription_admin_actions` rows carrying `new_status = 'suspended'`: **0**. Nothing to reconcile. The new CHECK constrains `companies` and not that table, so old rows would not have failed either way, but there are none.
    - Companies on `status = 'trial'` with an approval suspension: **1**. That is behaviour change 4's entire population.
    - Companies with a future `trial_end` but not on `trial`: **0**.
  - **Change 4 is declared, not fixed, and the reasoning is worth keeping because the obvious fix is a trap.**
    - **What is actually lost is smaller than it looks.** `isInTrial` in `src/utils/planState.ts:128` reads `trial_end` alone and never `status`, and `effectivePlanOf` (`:190`) returns premium whenever it is true — so a reactivated company **keeps the trial's premium tier**. Two narrower things change: `readsAsTrial()` in `SuperAdminDashboard` requires `status = 'trial'`, so the company drops out of the platform's trial counters; and `buildPlanState`'s `active` branch resolves to `inactive` when the company is *also* unsubscribed, which `planAllows` (`:207`) turns into everything but the overview being locked. `get_active_company_id()` admits `active` and `trial` alike, so server access is identical.
    - **Why not record the prior status.** It is not "one column written where the audit row is assembled". It is a new piece of state whose invariant **every** writer of `companies.status` has to maintain — `platform_company_lifecycle` (five actions), `platform_company_set_status`, `platform_company_update`, `platform_company_set_approval` and the backfill. Miss one and reactivate restores a status from an arbitrary point in the past, silently, which is worse and less visible than the bug it replaces. Reading it back out of the audit log instead is worse still: the Team page is already folded from that log, which is why decision D4's retention must not ship before slice 3.12 — a second reader would make a retention job break reactivate too.
    - **Why not the `trial_end` heuristic either**, even though with 0 stale future trial dates it would be correct on today's data. It trades one reporting inaccuracy for its mirror: a company that is paid up but carries a stale future `trial_end` would come back as `trial` and be counted as a running trial on the dashboard, and `TrialBanner` would tell a paying customer it is in a trial — which is the exact copy #68 was sent to remove. It also changes `platform_company_lifecycle('reactivate')` for every company reactivated from CompaniesPage, to fix a counter for one.
    - **So: after reactivating that one company, its `status` goes back to `trial` by hand.** One row, once, and only if it is reactivated at all.
    - **The principled fix belongs with slice 3.13**, where the legacy routes are removed: the Freigaben table's *Sperren* should call `platform_company_lifecycle('suspend')` rather than `platform_company_set_approval('suspend')`, so there is **one** suspending path paired with **one** reactivate. That also removes the asymmetry this slice had to work around — lifecycle suspends the company's profiles and the other paths do not — and it is the change that makes recording the prior status worth doing, because there would then be a single place to record it.
  - **`price_cents` is written NULL by every path in U1.** The column and the rule are in place, but the backfill has no per-period price to recover — `companies` stores none and `company_subscriptions.monthly_price` is a rate, not what a period cost — and neither a redemption nor a manual grant knows one. **U2 inherits a coverage table with no prices, and filling them is its first job.**
  - **The daily re-run exists and is not wired.** `app_private.recompute_all_company_subscriptions()` is callable and nothing calls it. Wiring it into `deploy/otp-housekeeping.sh` is an operational step on the host (that file is installed by hand and a release does not replace it), and it was left out of U1 on purpose: a sweep moves a company from `active` to `expired` at its expiry, which is access-neutral but is still a change nobody asked this slice for.
  - **The migration fails if any company's access changes.** It computes `get_active_company_id()`'s own predicate for every profile and every company before and after, in the same transaction, asserts the buckets account for every row, and raises on any difference — naming up to twenty companies first. It also raises if a company gained an expiry (rule 1) or is still `active` past one (rule 2), and prints every stored-word correction it made.
  - **Two company states disappeared from the test kit**, and their absence is the assertion: `tests/rls/tenant-state.sql` went from twelve companies to ten and `tests/rls/matrix.sql` lost its `subsuspended` row, because neither value can be inserted any more. `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` is where the refusal is checked.
- **One subscription model (plan 3.F step U2), what to know.** `20260919170000_subscription_invoice_coverage.sql`. U1 left invoices affecting nothing because the owner had not given a grace period; he gave it on 17.09.2026, and U2 is those three rules. Read this before U3.
  1. **Paying an invoice extends access.** The paid invoice records coverage for the calendar month it names, and the recompute derives the company's state from it.
  2. **Grace: two business days after the due date, then access closes.** Business days so that a Friday due date closes on the Tuesday and not on the Sunday.
  3. **Cancelling an invoice withdraws the coverage it granted**, and keeps the invoice, the payment and the trail row exactly as they were.
  - **The period an invoice covers is the calendar month it names, and that is the only period information there is.** `subscription_invoices` carries `invoice_month`, `invoice_year` and `UNIQUE (company_id, invoice_month, invoice_year)` (`20260607125649:150-169`) and no `period_start`, `period_end` or `service_from`. The mapping is the U0 report's, character for character (`scripts/reports/subscription-conflicts.sql:134-143`): `make_date(invoice_year, invoice_month, 1)` to that day plus one month, both `AT TIME ZONE 'Europe/Berlin'`. A month boundary read in UTC would move every period by an hour or two and file a payment made late on the last day of a month in the wrong one.
  - **`company_subscriptions.starts_at`/`.ends_at` are not consulted, and the one production invoice outside its own subscription's term (U0 class 08) is the reason to say so.** The rule is the invoice's own month, so if that invoice is ever paid, cancelled or re-opened through one of the three RPCs it grants exactly its own month and nothing is reconciled against the subscription row. The invoice is what the customer was billed and what the customer paid; the term is a second record U0 already reports as inconsistent, and trusting it would refuse a period that was paid for. **Which of the two records is wrong is the owner's call, not U2's.**
  - **"Due" is `subscription_invoices.due_date`, `date NOT NULL` (`20260607125649:161`), and no due *timestamp* was invented.** `GenerateInvoicesModal.tsx:78` writes it as `min(billing_day, 28)` of the invoiced month, so **the due date sits inside the month being invoiced, never after it** — which is why the grace has to be about an *unpaid* invoice, and why an unpaid invoice has to grant until it: a paid one already covers its whole month, which ends long after its own due date. `InvoiceDetailModal.tsx:41` and `SubscriptionBillingPage.tsx:196` read the column the same way. A `date` has no time of day, so the close is midnight Europe/Berlin and the customer keeps the whole of the due day and of each business day counted after it.
  - **So the rule is one coverage row per invoice, and it moves rather than multiplying:** open or overdue grants `[month start, due + 2 business days)`, paid grants `[month start, max(month end, due + 2 business days))`, cancelled is voided with `void_reason = 'invoice_canceled'`. The `max` is not decoration: `billing_day` may be the 28th, and 28 February plus two business days is in March, so without it marking an invoice paid would *shorten* what being open had already granted. The partial unique index U1 put on `(company_id, source, source_id)` is what keeps it one row.
  - **How many companies change admission at the moment of deploy: zero, and it is a property of the file rather than a claim about it.** The migration writes no coverage row, touches no invoice, recomputes no company, contains no `UPDATE public.companies` and no loop over `public.companies`. Self-check 7 fails the release if an `invoice` coverage row exists when it applies. There was therefore nothing for U1's before/after access gate to do here, which is why U2 does not carry one.
  - **And afterwards, an invoice row can only ever add.** The recompute takes `max(period_end)` over a company's live granting rows (`20260919150000:385-409`), so an invoice row can raise a company's expiry and never lower it. The two ways U2 takes access away are deliberate acts on one invoice — `platform_invoice_cancel` voids that invoice's row, `platform_invoice_mark_unpaid` shrinks it from the month back to the grace window — and neither can reach a row this release did not write, because no row exists for an older invoice. **"A company loses access two business days after its invoice was due" is therefore the steady state and not this release:** it becomes true for a company once its invoice rows are the furthest-reaching coverage it has, that is once the `migration` row U1 carried it in on has expired. On staging the U0 report counted **2 open invoices past due while access was active** (PR #21); neither company loses anything now, for exactly that reason.
  - **The sweep exists and is wired to nothing**, like U1's. `app_private.subscription_invoices_apply_all()` applies the rule to every invoice already in the table and answers how many it walked. Running it is an operational decision, not a deploy, and its effect is monotone in the same way — it writes a granting row for every invoice that is not cancelled, and a cancelled one has no row to void on a first run, so it can grant and never withdraw. **Wiring it, with `app_private.recompute_all_company_subscriptions()`, into `deploy/otp-housekeeping.sh` is what makes rule 2 continuous for an invoice nobody marks paid** — until then, an invoice generated by `GenerateInvoicesModal` and never touched grants nothing, so a company's access ends when its previous period does rather than at the grace. That file is installed on the host by hand and a release does not replace it.
  - **`price_cents` stops being NULL here, and the conversion happens in exactly one place**: `app_private.subscription_invoice_apply`, as `round(subscription_invoices.amount * 100)::bigint`. The invoice column is `numeric` euros, which is its unit since `20260607125649:157`. **It is the invoice's `amount`, not `cash_received_amount`** — the amount is what the period cost, and a period that cost 49,00 € does not become one that cost 50,00 € because the customer had no change. `platform_invoice_mark_paid` still converts the other way for its own column, `round(p_amount_cents::numeric / 100, 2)`, and that line is carried over untouched. `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` pays 15,00 € against an invoice of 20,00 € so that the coverage price cannot accidentally be the RPC's argument.
  - **The grace number lives in `app_private.subscription_grace_business_days()`, a `CONSTANT` in the one function that answers for it** — the shape every other limit in this codebase has (the OTP validity, the attempt count, the cooldown, `PASSWORD_PROOF_MAX_AGE_SECONDS`). No call site writes a 2, and self-check 5 fails a release where `app_private.invoice_grace_end` stops asking for it. **It is deliberately not a `platform_settings` key yet**, although the `invoice` document of `20260919120000` is where it belongs: that document's validator demands an exact key set (`20260919120000:296-298`), so adding a field means re-stating `app_private.platform_settings_problem_3_10`, raising the row's `version`, and editing four places in `tests/rls/settings.sql` that pin the key set and the version — for a knob with no screen to turn it, since the Einstellungen editor is 3.10's second half. **Moving it there is a one-function change when that screen is built**: replace the body with the read `app_private.signup_trial_days()` already demonstrates, and no caller moves.
  - **Business days are Monday to Friday and no public holiday is subtracted.** There is no holiday table in the database; the two that exist are TypeScript, cover different sets and disagree with each other every year (CLAUDE.md). Choosing between them would mean choosing a Bundesland per company, which nothing records. A public holiday therefore shortens the grace by one working day, in the customer's disfavour by at most a day or two.
  - **The three RPCs were re-stated, and here is what was carried over**, because this repository has lost a rule to a re-statement twice. All three: the `billing.manage` check through `app_private.platform_rpc_authorize` before anything is read, with a NULL company so a refusal is not attributed to a company the caller may not see; `SELECT … FOR NO KEY UPDATE` and `not_found` with a NULL company; the status guard answering `invalid_status` and naming the status found; exactly one `app_private.audit_write` row per call through `app_private.platform_rpc_finish`; the `{success, reason, error}` answer; `SECURITY DEFINER`, `VOLATILE`, `SET search_path = pg_catalog, public`. `mark_paid` additionally: the `invalid` branch for a NULL id or an amount at or below zero; both `paid` and `canceled` refused; the cents-to-euros conversion; `paid_at` defaulting to `now()`; `paid_by`, `collected_by` and the trimmed `receipt_note`; the trail row's action `marked_paid_cash` and its German wording with the actor's name, else address, else `Admin`. `mark_unpaid`: only a `paid` invoice; `paid_at`, `paid_by`, `collected_by` and `cash_received_amount` cleared and **`receipt_note` deliberately not**, which is the shipped behaviour; the action `marked_unpaid`. `cancel`: an already cancelled invoice refused; **only `status` written**, so the payment record survives, which is the half of rule 3 that does not change; the action `invoice_canceled`. The single addition to each is the call to the rule and the coverage outcome in the audit row's payload.
  - **The self-check is seven tripwires and nothing more**, following the division `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql`'s header sets out after U1 cost five kit rounds to text checks that matched their own comments. U1's `app_private.function_code()` and `app_private.companies_set_clauses()` are reused rather than re-implemented. Everything behavioural is section 5 of that kit file, which now covers: paying records the month and admits the company; the price is the invoice's amount in cents; a late payment still buys the whole month; an unpaid invoice grants until the grace and not past it; a Friday due date closes on the Tuesday; cancelling voids and keeps the record; re-opening shrinks without voiding; applying one invoice three times leaves one row; two invoices paid in one transaction leave the later end standing; and an open-ended U1 period is not capped by a payment.
  - **Dates in that kit section are of two kinds on purpose.** A claim about *access* uses the current calendar month, because a hard-coded 2026 stops meaning "the month has not ended". A claim about the *shape of a period* uses a fixed month whose weekdays are known (02.01.2026 is a Friday), because an unpaid period ends at `greatest(grace end, period start)` and a due date relative to today can collide with the period start — on the 25th of a month a due date 30 days back sits before the 1st, and the end is the clamp rather than the grace. Mixing the two is what would make a check pass on a Wednesday in March and fail on the first of a month.
  - **An assertion must not write and read the same rows in one expression**, and the kit cost a round proving it. `_sub_ok(desc, _sub_succeeded(_sub_do(… mark_paid …)) AND (SELECT period_end FROM subscription_coverage …) = …)` failed while the row it read was perfectly correct: an uncorrelated scalar sub-SELECT is planned as an **InitPlan**, and an InitPlan is not an operand of the `AND` — `ExecEvalAnd`'s left-to-right short-circuit orders the operands, it does not make the InitPlan wait for them. The read therefore ran before the payment it was meant to observe, found nothing, and the comparison was NULL, which `_sub_ok` reports as a failure indistinguishable from a wrong value. Do the write, then assert, in two statements. The one place where the read was a volatile function call rather than a sub-SELECT — so genuinely safe, since nothing hoists a volatile call — was split too: the rule is worth more without an exception. **`price_cents = 2000` in that row is the other thing the round proved**: U1 shipped the column NULL on every path and filling it was U2's first job, and the kit now pins that it lands.
  - **And they come from one clock.** `current_date` reads the session's TimeZone, which is UTC in the kit's container, while the invoice month is derived from `now() AT TIME ZONE 'Europe/Berlin'`. On the last night of a month those two name different months, so every relative date in section 5 is now taken from a single `v_today := (now() AT TIME ZONE 'Europe/Berlin')::date` and `current_date` appears nowhere.
  - **The kit caught a transposition the whole section was hiding behind**, and it is worth naming because the shape recurs: `_sub_invoice` listed its columns `invoice_month, invoice_year` and passed `p_year, p_month`, so 2026 went into `invoice_month` and the table's `CHECK (invoice_month BETWEEN 1 AND 12)` refused the first insert. The file aborts at the first error, so **all six grace cases after it had never executed** while the PR read as complete. The fix was the INSERT alone — the declaration and all nine call sites already agreed on year-first — and the columns were reordered to year-first too, so the arguments, the column list and the values now read in one order and there is nothing left to transpose. `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` is the only gate that executes any of this; a green vitest and a green build say nothing about it.
- **One subscription model (plan 3.F step U3), what to know.** `20260920120000_subscription_direct_write_wall.sql`. This is the last step: nothing writes the derived subscription columns directly any more. The five columns are `subscription_status`, `subscription_expires_at`, `subscription_started_at`, `subscription_duration_type` and `plan` on `companies`.
  - **Every direct write, found before anything was changed.** Searched: `src/`, `supabase/functions/`, every migration and production's schema fixture, `tests/`, `scripts/`, `deploy/` and `e2e/`.
    - **The browser: none.** Every company write in `src/` is an RPC. The one direct call left is `CompaniesPage.tsx:1090`, the hard delete (D13), which writes no column.
    - **Edge functions: none.** Their only `companies` call reads the name (`_shared/activation-code-delivery.ts:91`).
    - **SQL functions, in their current bodies: exactly the sanctioned writers.**
      - The recompute writes status, expiry and plan (`20260919150000:423-431`).
      - `platform_company_set_plan` writes plan (`:1504-1510`).
      - `redeem_activation_code` writes the start and the duration word (`:1254-1259`), and so does `update_company_subscription_manual` (`:1360-1364`).
      - `app_private.signup_insert_company` writes plan and `'inactive'`, on INSERT (`20260919120000:653-667`).
      - Every older body that assigned these columns had the same signature as the U1 restatement that replaced it, so none survives as a second overload: the fixture's `schema.sql:1603-1607` and `:1958-1963`, `20260621024136:174`, `20260621110244:161`, `20260621120417:162`, `20260621114909:53`, `20260916110010:358-362`, `20260916130000:735-741` and `:1023-1027`, and `20260916130020:429-433`.
      - The other `UPDATE companies` bodies write none of the five: `redeem_discount_code` (the discount columns), `platform_subscription_assign` (the car limit), and the lifecycle, approval, status, update and extend-trial RPCs.
    - **The table's own door was open.** `companies_update` and `companies_insert` (`20260917130000:585-592`) admit every column to a holder of `companies.lifecycle`, so an owner could PATCH or INSERT any of the five through PostgREST, past `platform_company_set_plan`'s audit row and past the coverage table. `service_role` bypasses RLS and holds the table's privileges, so an edge function could as well. Nothing used either door. **Closed by the trigger.**
    - **`e2e/setup-staging.sh:197-201` and `:252-256`** set `subscription_status = 'active'` and `subscription_expires_at = now() + 10 years` by hand, as `supabase_admin`, on staging's seven e2e companies. The next recompute would undo that. **It now calls `admit_company`**, which approves the company and records a ten-year period through `app_private.subscription_coverage_record(...)`, and records nothing for a company already covered for more than a year.
    - **`tests/rls/assertions.sql:256-257`** asserted that the platform owner *can* set `companies.plan` directly, and it left company A on premium for every later file. **It now asserts the opposite**, with a control that the row itself is still writable.
  - **The wall is a trigger, not a column REVOKE.** `refuse_direct_subscription_write`, BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE on `companies`, refuses a change to any of the five made as `anon`, `authenticated` or `service_role`, with SQLSTATE 42501. A REVOKE was rejected for three reasons.
    1. A column privilege is ignored while the table-level one is held. The wall would really be "revoke the table's UPDATE and INSERT, then grant every other column back", and any wholesale re-grant reopens it silently. This repository has shipped one: `20250930185334` runs `GRANT ALL ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO service_role`.
    2. A privilege refuses a column being *named*, not *changed*. The kit's `SET plan = plan` probes of the companies policy (`platform-authority.sql` §4) would have stopped at the privilege check, before the policy they exist to watch.
    3. "May not change what it already was" needs OLD, which only a trigger sees. That is the `pin_billing_company_id` precedent.
  - **How the sanctioned writers are told apart: `current_user`.** A SECURITY DEFINER function runs as its owner, and every sanctioned writer is one. PostgREST runs a request as one of the three API roles, the only roles the authenticator is a member of. No GUC and no JWT claim is read.
    - **What that cannot do** is tell one SECURITY DEFINER function from another, or stop a superuser at psql. The alternatives were rejected. The call stack as text (`PG_CONTEXT`) can be forged, because its frames carry each statement's text. A per-writer token means restating five writers, the move that has twice dropped a rule here.
    - **So the "only these writers" rule lives in the catalogue.** `app_private.subscription_column_writes()` reads every function in `public` and `app_private`, whatever its name, and the migration's self-check and `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` §8 both require its answer to equal the list above, in both directions. A new sanctioned writer goes into both lists in the same commit. §8 excludes only its own `_sub_%` helpers, by name, and plants a probe for every branch of the reader so it cannot pass by being blind.
    - **The reader reads text, and it has blind spots.** It cannot see dynamic SQL. A `;` inside a string literal ends a statement early for it, and `--` inside a string literal is stripped to the end of the line, so an assignment after either one is missed. Neither occurs in any writer today, and the trigger has none of these limits. The migration header lists them.
    - **The trigger function must stay SECURITY INVOKER** (self-check 2), and every sanctioned writer must stay SECURITY DEFINER (self-check 5). Otherwise the wall would refuse a customer's own redemption.
    - **The code-side rule is `src/utils/subscriptionDirectWrites.test.ts`.** It fails on a `.from('companies')` update, upsert or insert that names one of the five, or whose payload it cannot read. It was shown to fail by planting a violation and removing it again.
  - **How many companies change: zero, at deploy and afterwards.** The migration writes no row. It installs a trigger, a catalogue reader and checks. The one door it closes is used by nothing in the product.
  - **The two sweeps: U3 does not change what they would do.** It touches neither the recompute nor the invoice rule, so the number of companies U3 moves through them is 0 by construction. The staging dry run of 18.09 (zero changes) was taken before U2 went live on 19.09 and before any invoice was touched through it, though, so re-measure with the dry run below before installing either line. Read its answer for two things:
    1. **Any e2e company among those losing access.** A company that `company-signup` created after U1 and that the old script admitted by hand has no coverage, so the sweep would lock it and the suite with it. Run `cd e2e && npm run setup` once (it now records their coverage), then run the dry run again.
    2. **`plan_changes`.** A company whose every granting period has ended loses a tier set through `platform_company_set_plan` at the next recompute. The recompute's plan query has no end filter, so it reads the tier off the ended row, and set_plan carries the tier only onto periods that have not ended. Its own kit comment says "the recompute never reads it", which is true only while a live period exists. A redemption or a manual edit records the new tier first and is unaffected, but the daily sweep is not. **The candidate fix is one line**: add `AND (c.period_end IS NULL OR c.period_end > v_now)` to the recompute's plan query. It is a change to what the recompute derives, so it waits for the owner with this count, and U3 does not make it. **Made since, in `20260920150000` (entry below).** Once that is live, `plan_changes` counts only companies whose tier both bodies move — in practice an invoice period revived by a payment (`HOLE[revival]`).
  - **The dry run** (read-only in effect: it ends in ROLLBACK). **Superseded, once `20260921130000` is live, by the kept-void entry's dry run below.** Run it as `supabase_admin`, because `postgres` gets `permission denied` on `app_private` functions (measured on staging). Replace the directory with `/srv/PRO/deploy` for production.

```bash
cd /srv/PRO-staging/deploy && docker compose exec -T db psql -U supabase_admin -d postgres -X -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 <<'SQL'
BEGIN;
CREATE TEMP TABLE sweep_before AS
  SELECT id, name, subscription_status, subscription_expires_at, plan,
         coalesce(approval_status = 'approved' AND status IN ('active', 'trial')
                  AND subscription_status = 'active'
                  AND (subscription_expires_at IS NULL OR subscription_expires_at > now()), false) AS admits
    FROM public.companies;
SELECT app_private.subscription_invoices_apply_all()     AS invoices_walked;
SELECT app_private.recompute_all_company_subscriptions() AS companies_walked;
CREATE TEMP TABLE sweep_after AS
  SELECT id, subscription_status, subscription_expires_at, plan,
         coalesce(approval_status = 'approved' AND status IN ('active', 'trial')
                  AND subscription_status = 'active'
                  AND (subscription_expires_at IS NULL OR subscription_expires_at > now()), false) AS admits
    FROM public.companies;
SELECT count(*) FILTER (WHERE b.admits AND NOT a.admits) AS lose_access,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE NOT b.admits AND a.admits) AS gain_access,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE (b.subscription_status, b.subscription_expires_at)
                              IS DISTINCT FROM (a.subscription_status, a.subscription_expires_at)) AS stored_state_changes,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE b.plan IS DISTINCT FROM a.plan) AS plan_changes
  FROM sweep_before b JOIN sweep_after a USING (id);
SELECT b.name, b.admits AS admitted_before, a.admits AS admitted_after, b.plan AS plan_before, a.plan AS plan_after
  FROM sweep_before b JOIN sweep_after a USING (id)
 WHERE b.admits IS DISTINCT FROM a.admits OR b.plan IS DISTINCT FROM a.plan
 ORDER BY b.name;
ROLLBACK;
SQL
```

  - **The host lines, to copy once the owner has the numbers. Staging first, then production.** Put them in a new file, `/etc/cron.d/el-professor-subscriptions` (root, mode 0644). Each statement runs in its own transaction, invoices first so the recompute sees their periods, and psql stops at the first error.
    - **The timing.** `00:10` and `00:40` host time both fall after midnight Europe/Berlin, whether the host clock is UTC (01:10 or 02:10 Berlin) or Europe/Berlin. An unpaid invoice's grace closes at Berlin midnight, so it has always ended when the sweep runs.
    - **The guards** are the housekeeping script's own: the `PROJECT_NAME` check (with `tr -d '\r'`, and `-x` so `ya-mobility` cannot match `ya-mobility-staging`) and a `flock` against overlap.

```
10 0 * * *  root cd /srv/PRO-staging/deploy && tr -d '\r' < .env | grep -qx 'PROJECT_NAME=ya-mobility-staging' && flock -n /run/lock/el-professor-subscriptions-staging.lock docker compose exec -T db psql -U supabase_admin -d postgres -X -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -qtA -c "SELECT 'invoices=' || app_private.subscription_invoices_apply_all()" -c "SELECT 'companies=' || app_private.recompute_all_company_subscriptions()" < /dev/null 2>&1 | logger -t el-professor-subscriptions
40 0 * * *  root cd /srv/PRO/deploy && tr -d '\r' < .env | grep -qx 'PROJECT_NAME=ya-mobility' && flock -n /run/lock/el-professor-subscriptions-production.lock docker compose exec -T db psql -U supabase_admin -d postgres -X -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -qtA -c "SELECT 'invoices=' || app_private.subscription_invoices_apply_all()" -c "SELECT 'companies=' || app_private.recompute_all_company_subscriptions()" < /dev/null 2>&1 | logger -t el-professor-subscriptions
```

    Each run logs one line per statement: `invoices=N` and then `companies=N`, the counts walked, not the counts changed. Read them with `journalctl -t el-professor-subscriptions`. If the two sweeps ever move into `deploy/otp-housekeeping.sh` instead, the same two statements go into its `SQL` array as a third task, and the cron file then points at that script.
  - **Not verified here.** The RLS kit was not run. The files it has to execute are `supabase/migrations/20260920120000_subscription_direct_write_wall.sql`, `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` (header and §8) and `tests/rls/assertions.sql` (one probe turned around, one added). `e2e/setup-staging.sh` passed `bash -n` and has not run against staging.
- **The recompute keeps a tier the owner set once every period has ended (3.F fix, PR #88, in review), what to know.** `20260920150000_recompute_keeps_assigned_plan.sql`. The defect was live on production since U1.
  - **The defect, read off the code.** U1's recompute took the tier from the live granting row with the furthest end, with no condition on whether that end had passed (`20260919150000:414-421`), and wrote it with `plan = coalesce(v_plan, plan)` (`:426`). `platform_company_set_plan` carries a new tier only onto live granting rows that have not ended (`:1495-1502`). When every granting period of a company has ended — the recompute's third branch, `('expired', that end)` at `:402-403` — set_plan carries the tier onto nothing and the recompute reads the old tier off the ended row. The next recompute wrote it back: any `subscription_coverage_record` write (a redemption, a manual edit), any invoice RPC, and the nightly sweep once wired.
  - **The rule chosen: the recompute derives a tier only from a live granting period that has not ended** — `period_end IS NULL OR period_end > now()`, the exact predicate set_plan carries onto, and no term on `period_start`. With none, `plan` stands, as it already did for a company with no coverage. The code change is that one predicate; every other line of the body is U1's, and the PR lists each behaviour and where it is.
  - **Why it is safe.** The tier query orders open-ended rows first and then by end, so whenever a period that has not ended exists, the old body and the new one pick the same row. They differ for exactly one population: companies whose every granting period with a tier has ended. For them the old body wrote the ended period's tier and the new one writes nothing.
  - **Why not the other candidate.** Having set_plan record something the recompute respects means a new column and a second restated writer. Stamping the tier onto ended periods rewrites history, which U1 ruled out and section 3c pins.
  - **The two rules that had to hold still hold.** A company with no coverage keeps its plan, and an early renewal that has not begun still carries the upgrade through the sweep: section 9 asserts both, 3c still asserts the original early-renewal case, and a fourth check shows a period that has not begun still decides the tier.
  - **U3's wall and catalogue are unaffected.** The function stays SECURITY DEFINER and owned by the migration's role, so the trigger lets it through by `current_user`. It still writes `plan`, `subscription_status` and `subscription_expires_at` in one UPDATE, so `app_private.subscription_column_writes()` finds the same three entries and §8g's list needs no change. The migration's self-check asks the reader for exactly those three.
  - **What changes at deploy: nothing, for anybody.** The file replaces one function body and writes no row. It recomputes no company, so no `plan`, status or expiry moves while it applies. **Afterwards**, a company in the population above keeps its `plan` at every recompute. **A company the defect has already reverted is not restored**: its column already holds the old tier and the new body leaves it there. The owner sets it again on the screen, **after** this release is live, or the next recompute reverts it again.
  - **The blast radius, to run on production before the release.** Read-only, in a `READ ONLY` transaction that ends in ROLLBACK, and it calls no function, so `postgres` can run it as well as `supabase_admin`. Query 1 lists the companies whose tier the live body would revert at the next recompute. The fix keeps those, and they are the rows where `fixed_body_writes = plan_now`. A row where `fixed_body_writes` differs from `plan_now` is a company both bodies would move, which is the revival path below. Query 2 lists companies whose most recent owner-set tier is not the one they hold now. That is the defect having already fired, or a direct write before U3; the owner re-sets those after the release.

```bash
cd /srv/PRO/deploy && docker compose exec -T db psql -U supabase_admin -d postgres -X -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 <<'SQL'
BEGIN READ ONLY;

-- 1. The recompute's tier choice, by the live body and by the fixed one.
WITH tiered AS (
  SELECT c.company_id, c.plan, c.period_end, c.created_at
    FROM public.subscription_coverage c
   WHERE c.status = 'active' AND c.grants_access AND c.plan IS NOT NULL
),
live_body AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT ON (t.company_id) t.company_id, t.plan, t.period_end
    FROM tiered t
   ORDER BY t.company_id, (t.period_end IS NULL) DESC, t.period_end DESC, t.created_at DESC
),
fixed_body AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT ON (t.company_id) t.company_id, t.plan
    FROM tiered t
   WHERE t.period_end IS NULL OR t.period_end > now()
   ORDER BY t.company_id, (t.period_end IS NULL) DESC, t.period_end DESC, t.created_at DESC
)
SELECT co.name, co.id, co.status, co.subscription_status, co.subscription_expires_at,
       co.plan                     AS plan_now,
       l.plan                      AS live_body_writes,
       l.period_end                AS read_off_period_ending,
       coalesce(f.plan, co.plan)   AS fixed_body_writes
  FROM public.companies co
  JOIN live_body l ON l.company_id = co.id
  LEFT JOIN fixed_body f ON f.company_id = co.id
 WHERE l.plan IS DISTINCT FROM co.plan
 ORDER BY co.name;

-- 2. The defect having already fired: the tier the owner last set is not the
--    one the company holds now.
SELECT co.name, s.company_id, s.occurred_at AS last_set_at,
       s.after_value ->> 'plan' AS tier_owner_set, co.plan AS plan_now,
       co.subscription_status, co.subscription_expires_at
  FROM (SELECT DISTINCT ON (a.company_id) a.company_id, a.occurred_at, a.after_value
          FROM app_private.platform_audit_log a
         WHERE a.action = 'billing.set_plan' AND a.outcome = 'ok' AND a.company_id IS NOT NULL
         ORDER BY a.company_id, a.occurred_at DESC, a.id DESC) s
  JOIN public.companies co ON co.id = s.company_id
 WHERE (s.after_value ->> 'plan') IS DISTINCT FROM co.plan
 ORDER BY s.occurred_at DESC;

ROLLBACK;
SQL
```

  - **What it does not close, both in U2's `app_private.subscription_invoice_apply`, found by reading and not by execution:**
    1. **A revived period keeps its old tier (`HOLE[revival]`).** The function moves an invoice's row in place and never rewrites its tier, by design (`20260919170000:321-325`, `:412-421`). Take an unpaid invoice whose grace has passed: its row has ended at the old tier, and set_plan leaves it there. Marking the invoice paid later moves the row's end to the month's end. The row has then not ended, so it decides, and the tier goes back. It is realistic once the invoice sweep runs: an overdue invoice, an upgrade agreed with the customer, then the payment. `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` §9b records it as today's behaviour. **Candidate fix, the owner's call because it changes U2's "tier written at creation" rule:** when `subscription_invoice_apply` moves a row that was void or ended into one that has not ended, give it the company's current tier. That is set_plan's own rule, applied at the moment the period comes back.
    2. **A manual edit does not hold against a later apply of an invoice.** `update_company_subscription_manual` voids every live row with `p_replace` (`20260919150000:514-520`), invoice rows included. `subscription_invoice_apply` looks the invoice's row up without a status filter (`20260919170000:358-362`) and sets it back to `status = 'active'` (`:413-421`). So once the invoice sweep is wired, an administrator's manual `expired` or `inactive` on a company with a paid invoice for the current month is undone the next night, and the company is admitted again. **This one is about access, not only the tier.** Decide it before the invoice sweep's cron line goes in. Nothing tests it yet. **Fixed in `20260921130000`** (entry below).
  - **Verified in a stand-in, not in the kit.** The real RLS kit (`supabase/postgres:17`, on the VPS) was not run. It has to execute `supabase/migrations/20260920150000_recompute_keeps_assigned_plan.sql` and `tests/rls/subscriptions.sql` (header, one comment in §3c, and §9).
    - **The stand-in** was a throwaway PostgreSQL 16.6 cluster from laragon, in the scratchpad, on its own port and deleted afterwards. It loaded the schema fixture, the storage shim, all 43 migrations from `20260908` (after rebasing onto PRs #87 and #89), both seeds, and every `tests/rls` file except `salary-cash.sql`, which it cannot run (psql `\gset`). Everything passed. That includes this migration's self-check and all 182 checks `subscriptions.sql` prints, the 18 new ones among them.
    - **The negative control:** the same tests with this migration removed, so on U1's body, fail at "the next recompute leaves the owner's premium standing". That reproduces the defect by execution.
    - **Two dry checks as well:** the self-check's regexes and U3's reader were checked against the function body with a Node simulation of `app_private.function_code()`.
- **Re-applying an invoice keeps a period an administrator replaced (3.F fix, PR #96, branch `fix/invoice-reapply-keeps-void`, migration `20260921130000`), what to know.** This is what blocked the nightly sweeps.
  - **The defect**, live on production since U2: `app_private.subscription_invoice_apply` set an invoice's coverage row back to active whatever had voided it. A manual `expired` or `inactive` voids every live row as `replaced`, so the next re-apply of a paid invoice — the sweep, a direct call, or `platform_invoice_mark_unpaid` — admitted the company again.
  - **The rule now.** A voided invoice period comes back only when (1) its own cancellation voided it (`void_reason = 'invoice_canceled'`) and the invoice is no longer cancelled, or (2) it was `replaced` and the call is an explicit payment: `platform_invoice_mark_paid`, the one caller that passes `p_reinstate => true`. Everything else answers `superseded` and writes nothing. Case (2) keeps U2's rule 1, "paying extends access", for a payment entered after a manual edit.
  - **The two voids are told apart by `void_reason`**, recorded when the row was voided. Only the cancel branch writes `invoice_canceled`, and only `update_company_subscription_manual` writes `replaced`. Neither relabels the other's rows, because the cancel branch and `p_replace` both void live rows only. §10b pins the cancel-then-un-cancel sequence.
  - **The signature moved from `(uuid)` to `(uuid, boolean DEFAULT false)`.** The old function is dropped. The sweep, `mark_unpaid` and `cancel` keep calling it with one argument and were not restated. `mark_paid` was restated with that one argument added, and is otherwise character for character U2's. The function now locks the company before it reads the coverage row, which is the manual edit's lock order.
  - **The self-check fails a release when:** the old overload survives; a function other than `mark_paid` passes a second argument; the company lock no longer comes first; or U3's catalogue no longer answers exactly its list.
  - **What it does not close.**
    - **`HOLE[revival]` (§9b).** That row had ended; it was never voided. Unchanged, and the tag stays.
    - **An invoice with no coverage row yet.** The sweep's first run grants it, whatever a manual edit said since. Query 5 below lists these.
    - **An un-cancel made before a manual edit and applied after it.** This needs a PATCH through the invoices table's own UPDATE policy, and no screen makes one.
  - **At deploy: nothing moves.** No row is written and no company is recomputed. The header lists every rule of the old body and where it is now.
  - **Verified in a local throwaway `supabase/postgres:17.6.1.170` container, not on the VPS.** The full kit passed on the base before the rebase onto `04be061` (staging at `85d17b9`, without 3.13a's two migrations): every file, and 220 checks in `subscriptions.sql`, 38 of them in §10. The negative control ran the same kit without this migration, on U2's body, with §5f's signature line put back. It fails at §10a, "the sweep leaves the replaced period void". The real kit has not run.
  - **The dry run before installing the sweeps, staging first.** It supersedes the U3 entry's. Run it once `20260921130000` is live on that environment, as `supabase_admin`, at a quiet moment: it holds a row lock on every company until its ROLLBACK. Replace the directory with `/srv/PRO/deploy` for production. Send the owner its four numbers, the names in query 2 and every line of query 5. **Query 3 must show no `revived (replaced)` line, and query 4's two numbers must be equal.** The cron lines are the U3 entry's, unchanged. It was run once against a local throwaway database seeded with three companies, one per case, and read as intended. The replaced period stayed void and its company stayed out of query 2. The invoice nobody had applied showed in query 3 as `first row` and in query 5. The un-cancelled invoice showed as `revived (invoice_canceled)`.

```bash
cd /srv/PRO-staging/deploy && docker compose exec -T db psql -U supabase_admin -d postgres -X -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 <<'SQL'
BEGIN;

-- 0. Only with the fix live. Without it the sweep revives replaced periods, and
--    this dry run would measure the defect rather than the sweep.
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF to_regprocedure('app_private.subscription_invoice_apply(uuid,boolean)') IS NULL THEN
    RAISE EXCEPTION '20260921130000 is not applied here: do not install the sweep';
  END IF;
END $$;

CREATE TEMP TABLE sweep_before AS
  SELECT id, name, subscription_status, subscription_expires_at, plan,
         coalesce(approval_status = 'approved' AND status IN ('active', 'trial')
                  AND subscription_status = 'active'
                  AND (subscription_expires_at IS NULL OR subscription_expires_at > now()), false) AS admits
    FROM public.companies;
CREATE TEMP TABLE cov_before AS
  SELECT id, status, void_reason, period_end
    FROM public.subscription_coverage
   WHERE source = 'invoice';

SELECT app_private.subscription_invoices_apply_all()     AS invoices_walked;
SELECT app_private.recompute_all_company_subscriptions() AS companies_walked;

CREATE TEMP TABLE sweep_after AS
  SELECT id, subscription_status, subscription_expires_at, plan,
         coalesce(approval_status = 'approved' AND status IN ('active', 'trial')
                  AND subscription_status = 'active'
                  AND (subscription_expires_at IS NULL OR subscription_expires_at > now()), false) AS admits
    FROM public.companies;

-- 1. The four numbers.
SELECT count(*) FILTER (WHERE b.admits AND NOT a.admits) AS lose_access,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE NOT b.admits AND a.admits) AS gain_access,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE (b.subscription_status, b.subscription_expires_at)
                              IS DISTINCT FROM (a.subscription_status, a.subscription_expires_at)) AS stored_state_changes,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE b.plan IS DISTINCT FROM a.plan) AS plan_changes
  FROM sweep_before b JOIN sweep_after a USING (id);

-- 2. Every company whose admission or tier moves, by name.
SELECT b.name, b.admits AS admitted_before, a.admits AS admitted_after, b.plan AS plan_before, a.plan AS plan_after
  FROM sweep_before b JOIN sweep_after a USING (id)
 WHERE b.admits IS DISTINCT FROM a.admits OR b.plan IS DISTINCT FROM a.plan
 ORDER BY b.name;

-- 3. What the invoice half did, one line per invoice period it wrote: a first
--    row, a period its own cancellation voided coming back, or a live period
--    moved. A replaced period coming back here is the defect, and must not
--    appear.
SELECT co.name, i.invoice_number, i.status AS invoice_status,
       CASE WHEN b.id IS NULL                          THEN 'first row'
            WHEN b.status = 'void' AND c.status = 'active' THEN 'revived (' || b.void_reason || ')'
            WHEN c.status = 'void'                     THEN 'voided (' || c.void_reason || ')'
            ELSE 'moved' END                          AS what,
       b.period_end AS end_before, c.period_end AS end_after
  FROM public.subscription_coverage c
  JOIN public.subscription_invoices i ON i.id = c.source_id
  JOIN public.companies co            ON co.id = c.company_id
  LEFT JOIN cov_before b              ON b.id = c.id
 WHERE c.source = 'invoice'
   AND (b.id IS NULL
        OR (b.status, b.void_reason, b.period_end) IS DISTINCT FROM (c.status, c.void_reason, c.period_end))
 ORDER BY co.name, i.invoice_number;

-- 4. The fix at work: replaced periods the sweep left void. Must equal the
--    count before; a lower number here is the defect.
SELECT (SELECT count(*) FROM cov_before WHERE status = 'void' AND void_reason = 'replaced') AS replaced_before,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM public.subscription_coverage
```
